I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 20 / computer_numerically_controlled_tool_operator_or_programmer / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before I leave for school I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror and my phone camera, fixing my hair or changing shirts to hide the angle, asking my mom if it looks weird, and sometimes I do it so long that I miss lab or show up too wiped out to focus.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.

But it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.

Before I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.

The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks. It feels like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes. People tell me they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first before they notice anything about my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.\n\nBut it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks. It feels like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes. People tell me they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first before they notice anything about my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.\n\nBut it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks. It feels like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes. People tell me they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first before they notice anything about my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.\n\nBut it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep checking until it feels like I can leave.\n\nLike before school I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then my phone camera, fix my hair, change shirts if I think it hides the angle better… and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. I can get stuck doing that for so long that I miss lab or I show up already drained and can’t focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks. It feels like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes. People tell me they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first before they notice anything about my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.\n\nBut it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep checking until it feels like I can leave.\n\nLike before school I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then my phone camera, fix my hair, change shirts if I think it hides the angle better… and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. I can get stuck doing that for so long that I miss lab or I show up already drained and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the same way every time to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not exactly the same way every time, no. It’s just… before I leave for school I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair or change shirts if I think it hides the angle better.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep checking until it feels like I can leave.\n\nLike before school I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then my phone camera, fix my hair, change shirts if I think it hides the angle better… and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. I can get stuck doing that for so long that I miss lab or I show up already drained and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the same way every time to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not exactly the same way every time, no. It’s just… before I leave for school I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair or change shirts if I think it hides the angle better.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. And I can lose so much time doing that that I miss lab, or I get there and I’m already too wiped out to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something is unfinished or not quite right if you don't check your face in your usual way before leaving for school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does feel unfinished, I guess. Like if I don’t do the usual mirror check and then check my phone camera, I feel like I can’t really leave yet.\n\nI’ll start fixing my hair or changing shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep checking until it feels like I can leave.\n\nLike before school I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then my phone camera, fix my hair, change shirts if I think it hides the angle better… and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. I can get stuck doing that for so long that I miss lab or I show up already drained and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the same way every time to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not exactly the same way every time, no. It’s just… before I leave for school I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair or change shirts if I think it hides the angle better.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. And I can lose so much time doing that that I miss lab, or I get there and I’m already too wiped out to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something is unfinished or not quite right if you don't check your face in your usual way before leaving for school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does feel unfinished, I guess. Like if I don’t do the usual mirror check and then check my phone camera, I feel like I can’t really leave yet.\n\nI’ll start fixing my hair or changing shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still check, yeah. It’s not only about people seeing me, it’s more like I can’t leave it alone until it looks less wrong to me.\n\nEven if I was alone, I’d still end up in the bathroom mirror or using my phone camera, fixing my hair or changing shirts to hide that angle. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks. It feels like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes. People tell me they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first before they notice anything about my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.\n\nBut it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. And I can lose so much time doing that that I miss lab, or I get there and I’m already too wiped out to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something is unfinished or not quite right if you don't check your face in your usual way before leaving for school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does feel unfinished, I guess. Like if I don’t do the usual mirror check and then check my phone camera, I feel like I can’t really leave yet.\n\nI’ll start fixing my hair or changing shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still check, yeah. It’s not only about people seeing me, it’s more like I can’t leave it alone until it looks less wrong to me.\n\nEven if I was alone, I’d still end up in the bathroom mirror or using my phone camera, fixing my hair or changing shirts to hide that angle. When I’m leaving for school is when it gets bad, and sometimes I spend so long doing it that I miss lab or I get there already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the left side of your face being crooked is also bothersome because it doesn't look symmetrical to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a big part of it. It just doesn’t look even to me. The left side looks off, like my nose and jaw are kind of crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks. It feels like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes. People tell me they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first before they notice anything about my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.\n\nBut it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep checking until it feels like I can leave.\n\nLike before school I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then my phone camera, fix my hair, change shirts if I think it hides the angle better… and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. I can get stuck doing that for so long that I miss lab or I show up already drained and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the same way every time to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not exactly the same way every time, no. I’ll fix my hair or change shirts if I think it hides the angle better.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. And I can lose so much time doing that that I miss lab, or I get there and I’m already too wiped out to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something is unfinished or not quite right if you don't check your face in your usual way before leaving for school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does feel unfinished, I guess. Like if I don’t do the usual mirror check and then check my phone camera, I feel like I can’t really leave yet.\n\nI’ll start fixing my hair or changing shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still check, yeah. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still check, yeah. It’s not only about people seeing me, it’s more like I can’t leave it alone until it looks less wrong to me.\n\nEven if I was alone, I’d still end up in the bathroom mirror or using my phone camera, fixing my hair or changing shirts to hide that angle. When I’m leaving for school is when it gets bad, and sometimes I spend so long doing it that I miss lab or I get there already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the left side of your face being crooked is also bothersome because it doesn't look symmetrical to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a big part of it. It just doesn’t look even to me. The left side looks off, like my nose and jaw are kind of crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to check and fix your face more about making it look symmetrical or about hiding the perceived flaw?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but mostly I’m trying to hide it or make it less noticeable. Before I leave for school, I get stuck checking in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera, trying to see if that angle looks wrong.\n\nThen I’ll mess with my hair or change shirts to cover it better, and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab or I get there already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks. It feels like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes. People tell me they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first before they notice anything about my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do that a lot. Before I leave for school, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom mirror checking the left side of my face, then I’ll check it again on my phone camera. I keep fixing my hair or changing shirts because I’m trying to make that angle less obvious.\n\nI also ask my mom if it looks weird, sometimes more than once. It can go on way too long, to the point where I miss lab or I get there late and I’m already so drained I can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance in the mirror or asking your mom about it helps to reduce your worries about your face?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like when I check the mirror or my phone, I feel like I’m trying to make sure it’s not as bad as I think. Or if my mom says it looks fine, I’ll calm down for a minute.\n\nBut it doesn’t really last. I end up checking again, fixing my hair, changing shirts, trying to hide that angle. Sometimes it takes so long before school that I miss lab or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stressed about school and stuff, but I haven’t noticed other thoughts that repeat in that same way. It’s mostly the face thing that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something really bad would happen, exactly. It’s more like I feel like I can’t leave until I check it and try to fix it. If I don’t look in the mirror or my phone camera, or fix my hair or change shirts, I get really uneasy, like everyone’s going to notice that angle.\n\nSo I end up doing it before school, and sometimes it takes so long I miss lab or show up already wiped out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more about how you feel when you think about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I feel like it actually looks different, at least to me. The left side of my face just looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious, like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would think less of you or treat you differently if they noticed the imperfections you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think that’s a big part of it. If they notice it, I feel like they’re going to think I look weird or not take me as seriously.\n\nLike in the CNC lab, I worry people see the left side of my face first—like my nose or jaw being crooked—before they notice my work. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could stop checking your face if you didn't care what other people thought about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still have trouble stopping. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. It bothers me because it doesn’t look right to me.\n\nI keep seeing the left side of my face as off, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s there, and like everyone in the CNC lab would notice that before anything else about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to be perfect, or is it more about avoiding looking abnormal?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not looking abnormal, I think. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no. It’s more that I keep checking until it feels like I can leave.\n\nLike before school I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then my phone camera, fix my hair, change shirts if I think it hides the angle better… and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. I can get stuck doing that for so long that I miss lab or I show up already drained and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the same way every time to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not exactly the same way every time, no. It’s just… before I leave for school I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair or change shirts if I think it hides the angle better.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. And I can lose so much time doing that that I miss lab, or I get there and I’m already too wiped out to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something is unfinished or not quite right if you don't check your face in your usual way before leaving for school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does feel unfinished, I guess. Like if I don’t do the usual mirror check and then check my phone camera, I feel like I can’t really leave yet.\n\nI’ll start fixing my hair or changing shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still check, yeah. It’s not only about people seeing me, it’s more like I can’t leave it alone until it looks less wrong to me.\n\nEven if I was alone, I’d still end up in the bathroom mirror or using my phone camera, fixing my hair or changing shirts to hide that angle. When I’m leaving for school is when it gets bad, and sometimes I spend so long doing it that I miss lab or I get there already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the left side of your face being crooked is also bothersome because it doesn't look symmetrical to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a big part of it. It just doesn’t look even to me. The left side looks off, like my nose and jaw are kind of crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to check and fix your face more about making it look symmetrical or about hiding the perceived flaw?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but mostly I’m trying to hide it or make it less noticeable. Before I leave for school, I get stuck checking in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera, trying to see if that angle looks wrong.\n\nThen I’ll mess with my hair or change shirts to cover it better, and sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there too drained to focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And I can lose so much time doing that that I miss lab, or I get there and I’m already too wiped out to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something is unfinished or not quite right if you don't check your face in your usual way before leaving for school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does feel unfinished, I guess. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still check, yeah. When I’m leaving for school is when it gets bad, and sometimes I spend so long doing it that I miss lab or I get there already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the left side of your face being crooked is also bothersome because it doesn't look symmetrical to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a big part of it. It just doesn’t look even to me. The left side looks off, like my nose and jaw are kind of crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to check and fix your face more about making it look symmetrical or about hiding the perceived flaw?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but mostly I’m trying to hide it or make it less noticeable. Maybe it would be less if I didn’t care what people thought, but I get stuck in it anyway.\n\nBefore I leave for school, I’ll keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror and then on my phone camera. I’ll fix my hair, change shirts to try to hide the angle, and sometimes ask my mom if it looks weird. It can go on so long that I miss lab or get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way, not just to avoid people noticing something wrong, but to look right or perfect to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only about other people noticing. Like, I’m not trying to look perfect exactly — I just can’t stop feeling like the left side of my face looks wrong.\n\nMy nose and jaw seem crooked to me in ID photos or on the online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Not really in a strict order, I guess. It’s more like I get stuck doing the same checks over and over until it feels okay enough to leave.\n\nI’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then check my phone camera, fix my hair, maybe change shirts to try to hide the angle. Sometimes I ask my mom if it looks weird. It can take so long that I miss lab, or I get there already exhausted and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no. It can go on so long that I miss lab, or I get there already worn out and can’t focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and nobody would see you?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still check, yeah. When I’m leaving for school is when it gets bad, and sometimes I spend so long doing it that I miss lab or I get there already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the left side of your face being crooked is also bothersome because it doesn't look symmetrical to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s a big part of it.
